this is my idea

7 Jun
This is a well-structured product concept. Let me reflect back what makes it strong and where the real challenges are.

What's genuinely good here

The two-LLM-job discipline is the right call. Most AI products overcall LLMs and end up with hallucination-prone outputs. You've correctly identified the two places where language understanding is actually needed — parsing and prose generation — and made everything in between deterministic. That's architecturally sound.

The HITM gate logic is elegant. Asking questions only when there's a genuine tie, batching them, and capping at 3 is the right UX instinct. Most systems either ask too much upfront or never ask at all.

The two-moat framing is honest and defensible. Document-extracted profiles + hand-crafted composition knowledge is a real differentiation if you execute it.

Where the real difficulty lives

The cheat sheets sound simple but they're not. "Fintech" isn't one thing — a payment processor, a trading platform, a neobank, and a lending SaaS have almost nothing in common architecturally. Your classifier will need sub-classification or the cheat sheets become either too generic to be useful or too numerous to maintain.

The component profiles are the hardest dependency. Extracting structured, queryable specs from Cisco, AWS, and ISACA documents is months of work if done rigorously. AWS alone changes pricing and service availability constantly. You need a refresh strategy baked in from day one, not bolted on later.

The diagram generation step is deceptively hard. Python diagrams library will get you something, but "professional, not auto-generated looking" is a high bar. Real architecture diagrams have spatial logic — groupings, flow direction, visual weight. Auto-layout engines don't respect this. Budget significant time here.

The thing worth questioning

You've designed a system that outputs a finished architecture. But most real architecture decisions involve tradeoffs that even expert architects disagree on. If your system presents one confident answer, you'll either be wrong in ways that erode trust, or you'll hedge so much the output becomes generic. Consider whether the output should be a recommendation with explicit tradeoff visibility rather than a single "correct" design.

Sharpest next question

Who is the actual user — an architect who validates the output, a developer who follows it, or a buyer who uses it to scope a project? That answer changes almost everything about what the output needs to be.

i will first go for arcitecture creation like eraser.io then in paid version we will go niche arch creation

7 Jun
Good pivot. You're essentially saying: Phase 1 = horizontal (any architecture, AI-assisted, like Eraser), Phase 2 = vertical (domain-specific, the full system you described).

That's the right sequencing. Here's why it works and what to watch:

Why this order makes sense

Eraser's core loop is simple — natural language → diagram. You can ship a credible v1 without cheat sheets, component profiles, or compliance engines. You learn what users actually ask for before you hand-craft anything. The niche system in your document is expensive to build blind; Phase 1 gives you real data to build it right.

What you need to beat Eraser at their own game

Eraser already exists and has traction. You can't win on "also does diagrams." You need one sharp differentiator even in Phase 1. Looking at your screenshot — they generate generic boxes. Your edge could be:

Real service icons + specs on hover — when you drop an AWS ALB, it shows actual limits, pricing tier, compatibility notes pulled from your docs
Instant consistency warnings — even in freeform mode, flag "database is in public subnet" in real time
Architecture-aware layout — not just boxes and arrows, but auto-grouping into tiers, subnets, VPCs visually
Any one of these makes Phase 1 meaningfully different, not just another Eraser clone.

The trap to avoid

Building Phase 1 so generically that it doesn't set up Phase 2. Every design decision in Phase 1 should anticipate the niche engine — your component model, your document corpus, your rule engine should be started even if not surfaced to users yet. Phase 1 UI, Phase 2 brain building in parallel underneath.

Sharpest question now

What's your diagram rendering plan — are you wrapping an existing graph library (Mermaid, D3, Svelvet), building on a canvas library (Konva, Fabric), or licensing something? That decision shapes everything about how fast you can ship and how good the output looks.

7 Jun
Good. Let me map the whole system clearly.

What you're building in plain terms

User types a prompt → your system reads AWS/Cisco/compliance documents → generates a proper architecture diagram with a good UI.

Here's how every piece connects:

V

Connecting to visualize...
Click any box for a deeper explanation. Here's the plain-English breakdown of every layer:

User prompt — free text, any length, any specificity. Your system handles vague and detailed equally.

LLM #1 (input parser) — the only place AI is needed here. It reads messy natural language and outputs clean, structured JSON. App type, cloud, compliance needs, scale, budget, region. Ambiguous fields are flagged for later.

Classifier + cheat sheet router — pure rule-based code. Reads the JSON, matches to a domain (fintech, healthcare, etc.). Each cheat sheet is a curated pool of relevant services, compliance controls, and architecture patterns for that domain. No AI, no guessing.

Constraint engine + scorer — still pure code. Takes the cheat sheet pool and filters it against the parsed requirements. Hard filters eliminate incompatible options. Soft scoring ranks what's left. Every component slot (compute, database, load balancer, security) gets a ranked candidate list.

HITM gate — checks if any slot has a genuine tie. If two options score within a threshold of each other and the differentiator can't be resolved from context, it queues a question. All questions batch into one prompt to the user, maximum three, ordered by importance. Most runs won't trigger this at all.

Architecture assembler — combines the winning components into a structured architecture object. Tiers, services, compliance controls, certificates. This is the source of truth for everything downstream.

Consistency validator — rule engine checks the assembled architecture against domain rules. PCI-DSS without WAF? Auto-add WAF. Database in public subnet? Move it. High availability but single AZ? Enforce Multi-AZ. Flags tradeoffs it can't auto-resolve.

Outputs — two parallel jobs. Code renders the diagram. LLM #2 writes the report prose, but every sentence is grounded in retrieved document content — it phrases what the system already decided, it doesn't invent.

The core insight — only two things in this entire pipeline require AI. Everything in between is deterministic code. That's why the output is trustworthy.

What do you want to go deeper on — the document processing side, the cheat sheet structure, or the diagram rendering?

Before recommending a stack, a few quick questions:

Q: What's your primary language comfort?

Q: How do you want to deploy this?
A: well currently the things is that i cant do it on my desktop as i want the output on the window as in graphics and editable so had to do in hosting only but will go for vercel free hosting untill i develop it

Q: Do you want to build the diagram renderer yourself or use an existing tool?
A: Don't know the options

7 Jun
Good. Let me lay out the full stack recommendation for your situation.

V

Connecting to visualize...
Click any box for detail. Here's the reasoning behind each choice:

Frontend — Next.js on Vercel
You get free hosting, fast deploys, and it pairs perfectly with React Flow which is the best library for interactive, editable node-based diagrams in the browser. This is what handles the canvas where users see and edit their architecture diagram.

Backend — FastAPI on Railway
You're comfortable in Python, FastAPI is the cleanest Python framework for building APIs. It runs your constraint engine, HITM logic, rule validator, and orchestrates everything. Railway's free tier is enough to start. Vercel can't run Python servers, so this is a separate deployment — your Next.js frontend calls your FastAPI backend via API.

AI pipeline — Claude API + LangChain
Claude API for your two LLM jobs (input parser and report writer). LangChain handles the RAG pipeline — chunking your documents, retrieving relevant chunks, passing them to the LLM. Don't overbuild this early, LangChain's basics are enough.

Storage — Supabase
Free tier, gives you Postgres + pgvector (vector embeddings in the same database) + file storage (for saving generated diagrams). One service instead of three. This is where your document chunks, embeddings, component profiles, and user sessions all live.

Diagram renderer — React Flow in the browser
This is the key decision. Your original plan mentioned Python's diagrams library, but that generates static images on a server. React Flow renders interactive, editable, draggable diagrams directly in the browser — which is exactly what you want for "good UI" and "editable." Your backend sends a JSON architecture object, your frontend renders it as a live diagram.

The flow in one line: User types prompt → Next.js sends it to FastAPI → FastAPI runs the pipeline → returns architecture JSON → Next.js renders it with React Flow.

Want to start with setting up the project structure, or go straight into building the first piece (the input parser)?

Step 2 — Local setup on your machine

# Check you have Node installed

When it asks questions during setup, choose: TypeScript yes, Tailwind yes, App Router yes, everything else default.

Step 3 — Folder structure

Inside your archgen folder, create this structure:

archgen/
├── frontend/          ← this IS the Next.js app (rename app/ contents here)
├── backend/           ← create this folder manually
│   ├── main.py
│   ├── requirements.txt
│   └── .env
├── .env.local         ← for frontend secrets
└── README.md
Run this to create the backend folder:

bash
mkdir backend

cd ..

In backend/.env:

SUPABASE_KEY=
In .env.local (frontend root):

NEXT_PUBLIC_BACKEND_URL=http://localhost:8000
Supabase URL and key — leave blank for now, we'll fill these in the next step when we create the Supabase project.

Step 7 — Minimal FastAPI starter

Paste this into backend/main.py:

python
from fastapi import FastAPI

app = FastAPI()

app.add_middleware(
    CORSMiddleware,
    allow_origins=["http://localhost:3000"],
    allow_methods=["*"],
    allow_headers=["*"],
)

@app.get("/health")
def health():
    return {"status": "ok"}
Test it runs:

bash

Open http://localhost:8000/health — you should see {"status":"ok"}.

Perfect structure. Everything is in place.

Here's what we built and why:

What exists now:

app/ — your Next.js frontend. This is what users see in the browser. Vercel will deploy this.

backend/ — your Python FastAPI server. This runs your AI pipeline, constraint engine, all the logic. Railway will deploy this.

venv/ — isolated Python environment. Keeps your project's Python packages separate from your system Python. Always activate this before touching backend code.

node_modules/ — Next.js and its dependencies. Auto-managed by npm, never touch manually.

.env.local — secret keys for the frontend (never goes to GitHub).

.git/ — git is already initialized, ready to push to GitHub.

The separation that matters:

Browser (user)
     ↕  HTTP
Next.js — Vercel (free)       ← JavaScript world
     ↕  API calls
FastAPI — Railway (free)      ← Python world  ← your venv lives here
     ↕
Supabase                      ← database + file storage

Add backend starter code to main.py
Install Python dependencies in venv
Test both frontend and backend run locally
Create GitHub repo and push
Set up Supabase project and get keys
Deploy

what did the codes we ran did

7 Jun
Good question. Let me break down every command you ran:

python -m venv venv
Created an isolated Python environment inside a folder called venv/. Think of it as a clean box — any Python packages you install go inside this box, not globally on your Mac. Keeps your project dependencies separate from everything else on your machine.

source venv/bin/activate
Switched your terminal into that box. That's why you see (venv) at the start. Now when you run pip install, it installs inside the box, not system-wide.

mkdir backend
Created the backend folder manually.

touch main.py requirements.txt .env
Created three empty files inside backend. touch just creates a blank file if it doesn't exist.

touch .env.local
Created an empty file for frontend secrets at the root level.

npx create-next-app@latest . --typescript --tailwind --app --yes
This failed because the folder wasn't empty. Next.js refused to overwrite existing files.

The workaround — create in nexttemp then copy:

npx create-next-app@latest nexttemp — built the full Next.js project in a fresh empty folder called nexttemp
cp -r nexttemp/. basaltcode/ — copied everything from nexttemp into your basaltcode folder
rm -rf nexttemp — deleted the now-empty temp folder
What create-next-app actually did:

Created app/ — your pages and routes
Created public/ — static files like images
Created package.json — list of JS dependencies
Installed node_modules/ — downloaded all those dependencies
Set up Tailwind, TypeScript, ESLint configs
Ran git init — initialized a git repo
Everything ready. Want to move to writing main.py?

Now we start building the actual product.

First thing to build is the database schema in Supabase — we need tables for storing document chunks and embeddings before we can do anything with the AI pipeline.

Go to Supabase → click SQL Editor in the left sidebar. Ready to paste the schema?

opened

8 Jun
Paste this into the SQL editor and click "Run":

sql
-- Enable vector extension
create extension if not exists vector;

-- Store uploaded documents
create table documents (
  id uuid default gen_random_uuid() primary key,
  name text not null,
  source text,
  created_at timestamp default now()
);

-- Store document chunks with embeddings
create table document_chunks (
  id uuid default gen_random_uuid() primary key,
  document_id uuid references documents(id) on delete cascade,
  content text not null,
  embedding vector(1536),
  metadata jsonb,
  created_at timestamp default now()
);

-- Store generated architectures
create table architectures (
  id uuid default gen_random_uuid() primary key,
  prompt text not null,
  architecture_json jsonb not null,
  diagram_url text,
  report text,
  created_at timestamp default now()
);

-- Index for fast vector similarity search
create index on document_chunks 
using ivfflat (embedding vector_cosine_ops)
with (lists = 100);
Tell me what you see after running it — success or any error.

This query creates tables without enabling Row Level Security. Clients using anon or authenticated keys may be able to access these tables.
Choose whether to enable Row Level Security before running this query.

Now we start building the actual pipeline. First piece is the input parser — user types a prompt, your backend sends it to OpenAI, gets back structured JSON.

Update your backend/main.py with this:

python
from fastapi import FastAPI
from fastapi.middleware.cors import CORSMiddleware
from dotenv import load_dotenv
from openai import OpenAI
import os
import json

load_dotenv()

app = FastAPI()
client = OpenAI(api_key=os.getenv("OPENAI_API_KEY"))

app.add_middleware(
    CORSMiddleware,
    allow_origins=["http://localhost:3000"],
    allow_methods=["*"],
    allow_headers=["*"],
)

@app.get("/health")
def health():
    return {"status": "ok", "message": "Basalt backend running"}

@app.post("/parse")
async def parse_prompt(body: dict):
    prompt = body.get("prompt", "")
    
    response = client.chat.completions.create(
        model="gpt-4o-mini",
        messages=[
            {
                "role": "system",
                "content": """You are an architecture requirements parser. 
Extract structured information from the user's prompt and return ONLY valid JSON.
No explanation, no markdown, just raw JSON.

Return this exact structure:
{
  "app_type": "string (fintech/healthcare/ecommerce/saas/gaming/general)",
  "cloud": "string (aws/gcp/azure/hybrid/unspecified)",
  "scale": "string (low/medium/high/unspecified)",
  "budget": "string (low/medium/high/unspecified)",
  "compliance": ["array of compliance requirements like PCI-DSS, HIPAA etc"],
  "region": "string or unspecified",
  "on_prem": false,
  "ambiguous_fields": ["fields that were not clear from the prompt"]
}"""
            },
            {
                "role": "user",
                "content": prompt
            }
        ]
    )
    
    raw = response.choices[0].message.content
    parsed = json.loads(raw)
    return {"status": "success", "parsed": parsed}
Save the file. Uvicorn will auto-reload since we used --reload. Then test it:

bash
curl -X POST http://localhost:8000/parse \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"prompt": "fintech app on AWS with PCI-DSS compliance, high availability"}'
Paste what you get back.

(venv) SatvikMishra@MacBook-Pro ~/Desktop/basaltcode/backend % curl -X POST http://localhost:8000/parse \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"prompt": "fintech app on AWS with PCI-DSS compliance, high availability"}'
{"status":"success","parsed":{"app_type":"fintech","cloud":"aws","scale":"unspecified","budget":"unspecified","compliance":["PCI-DSS"],"region":"unspecified","on_prem":false,"ambiguous_fields":["scale","budget","region"]}}%                                                                                                     
(venv) SatvikMish

Pushed. Now let's build the classifier.

Create a new file:

bash
touch /Users/SatvikMishra/Desktop/basaltcode/backend/classifier.py
This is the cheat sheet router — pure Python, no LLM. It reads the parsed JSON and returns the right domain cheat sheet.


Ran a command, read a file
Ran a command, read a file
Download that file and put it in your backend/ folder.

Then add this to main.py — add the import at the top and the new endpoint at the bottom:

At the top of main.py add:

python
from classifier import classify
At the bottom of main.py add:

python
@app.post("/classify")
async def classify_prompt(body: dict):
    parsed = body.get("parsed", {})
    result = classify(parsed)
    return {"status": "success", "classified": result}
Then test it:

8 Jun
Yes, exactly. These are called reference architectures and design standards. Here's the full list:

The most important ones — these literally propose how to design systems:

Industrial & OT

ISA-95 (defines how enterprise and plant floor connect — the gold standard)
ISA-99 / IEC 62443 (proposes security zones and conduits for industrial networks)
Purdue Enterprise Reference Architecture (PERA) — the classic IT/OT layered model
NAMUR NE 153 (open architecture for process industry)
NAMUR NE 167 (cloud for process industry)
Open Group O-PAS standard (open process automation)
Network Architecture

Cisco SAFE Architecture (proposes exact security architecture for enterprise)
Cisco Validated Designs (CVD) — proposes exact network designs per industry
Cisco Industrial Ethernet design guides
NIST SP 1800 series (these are practice guides — propose exact implementations)
Zero Trust Architecture — NIST SP 800-207
Data Center Design

TIA-942 (proposes data center tiers and design)
ANSI/BICSI 002 (data center design standard)
Uptime Institute Tier Standard
Open Compute Project specifications
Cloud Architecture

AWS Well-Architected Framework (5 pillars — proposes how to build on AWS)
AWS Reference Architectures (per industry — exact diagrams)
Azure Architecture Center (Microsoft's reference designs)
Google Cloud Architecture Framework
TOGAF (The Open Group Architecture Framework — enterprise wide)
Security Architecture

SABSA framework (security architecture methodology)
Sherwood Applied Business Security Architecture
NIST Zero Trust Architecture SP 800-207
CIS Controls v8 (proposes exact security controls in order)
MITRE ATT&CK for ICS (attack patterns — tells you what to defend against)
MITRE D3FEND (defensive architecture proposals)
SAP Specific

SAP Reference Architecture for S/4HANA
SAP Activate methodology
SAP Enterprise Architecture Framework
SAP Landscape Transformation guide
Pharma Specific

ISPE GAMP 5 (proposes exact validation approach for pharma systems)
ISPE Baseline Guides (volumes 1-5 — propose facility and system design)
ISPE Good Practice Guide for process control
FDA Process Validation guidance
Manufacturing

ISA-88 (batch control standard — proposes exact batch architecture)
ISA-106 (procedural automation)
ISO 22400 (KPIs for manufacturing operations)
MESA Manufacturing Reference Architecture
Three that are absolute must-haves for your system:

ISA-95 — defines the 5-level manufacturing hierarchy. Every IT/OT architecture references this. Without it your cheat sheets have no backbone.

Purdue Model (PERA) — the diagram everyone draws when designing plant networks. Level 0 to Level 4. Your system must know this deeply.

NIST SP 800-82 — proposes exact security architecture for industrial control systems. Free PDF from NIST website.

These three together define how 90% of industrial architectures are structured. Everything else builds on top of them. Get these three first before anything else.

what is the flow of this basalt idea from entering prompt on iu to display of arch

9 Jun
Here's the complete flow:

V

Connecting to visualize...
Click any box for detail. Here's the plain English of every step:

1. User types prompt — anything from "pharma plant OT network" to a detailed 5-line description. Your UI accepts all of it.

2. LLM #1 parses it — OpenAI reads the free text and outputs clean JSON. Domain, compliance needs, scale, cloud, ambiguous fields flagged.

3. JSON sits as structured data — this travels through the rest of the pipeline as the source of truth.

4. Classifier routes to cheat sheet — pure Python, no AI. Reads the JSON, matches to your domain (Pharma / FMCG / IT-OT / Metal / SCADA). Pulls the right pool of components, standards, and patterns.

5. Constraint engine scores — filters and ranks every component slot against your rules and the RAG corpus. Hard filters first, soft scoring second.

6. HITM gate — if two components tie and context can't resolve it, asks the user one question. Batched, max 3. Most runs skip this entirely.

7. Architecture assembler — combines winning components into one architecture JSON object.

8. Consistency validator — checks rules. Missing firewall? Auto-add. Conflicting requirements? Flag to user.

9a. Diagram renderer — React Flow takes the JSON and draws the interactive, editable diagram in the browser.

9b. LLM #2 writes the report — every sentence grounded in retrieved document chunks from your corpus. No hallucination, only phrasing what the system already decided.

10. User gets — editable diagram they can move around, plus a PDF report with justifications and compliance mapping.

Come back with your documents and we build the RAG pipeline.

9 Jun
Good. Here's the search code:


Ran a command, read a file
Ran a command, read a file
Download and save to backend/search.py.

Now we need to create a database function in Supabase that does the vector similarity search. Go to Supabase → SQL Editor and run this:

sql
create or replace function match_chunks(
  query_embedding vector(1536),
  match_count int default 5
)
returns table(
  id uuid,
  content text,
  metadata jsonb,
  similarity float
)
language plpgsql
as $$
begin
  return query
  select
    document_chunks.id,
    document_chunks.content,
    document_chunks.metadata,
    1 - (document_chunks.embedding <=> query_embedding) as similarity
  from document_chunks
  order by document_chunks.embedding <=> query_embedding
  limit match_count;
end;
$$;
Tell me when it runs successfully.

    "pharmaceutical": "pharma",
    "drug": "pharma",

    "manufacturing": "metal",
}

def classify(parsed: dict) -> dict:
    app_type = parsed.get("app_type", "general").lower()
    app_type = APP_TYPE_ALIASES.get(app_type, app_type)
    if app_type not in CHEAT_SHEETS:
        app_type = "general"
        matched = False
    else:
        matched = True
    return {
        "app_type": app_type,
        "matched": matched,
        "cheat_sheet": CHEAT_SHEETS[app_type],
        "cloud": parsed.get("cloud", "aws"),
        "compliance": parsed.get("compliance", []),
        "scale": parsed.get("scale", "unspecified"),
        "budget": parsed.get("budget", "unspecified"),
        "ambiguous_fields": parsed.get("ambiguous_fields", [])
    }
EOF

9 Jun
Clear error — the match_chunks function isn't in Supabase's schema cache. Go to Supabase → SQL Editor and run this:

sql
drop function if exists match_chunks;

create or replace function match_chunks(
  query_embedding vector(1536),
  match_count int default 5
)
returns table(
  id uuid,
  content text,
  metadata jsonb,
  similarity float
)
language plpgsql
as $$
begin
  return query
  select
    document_chunks.id,
    document_chunks.content,
    document_chunks.metadata,
    1 - (document_chunks.embedding <=> query_embedding) as similarity
  from document_chunks
  order by document_chunks.embedding <=> query_embedding
  limit match_count;
end;
$$;
After it runs successfully, go to Supabase → Settings → API and click "Reload schema cache" button.

Then test again:

bash
curl -X POST http://localhost:8000/pipeline \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"prompt": "pharma plant OT network with FDA 21 CFR compliance on AWS"}'

9 Jun
match_chunks is there. The issue is the schema cache.

Go to Supabase → Settings (gear icon bottom left) → API → scroll down and look for a "Reload" or "Invalidate cache" button.

If you can't find it, the fastest fix is to update search.py to pass parameters differently. Run this in terminal:

bash
cat > /Users/SatvikMishra/Desktop/basaltcode/backend/search.py << 'EOF'
import os
from dotenv import load_dotenv
from supabase import create_client
from openai import OpenAI

load_dotenv()

supabase = create_client(os.getenv("SUPABASE_URL"), os.getenv("SUPABASE_KEY"))
openai_client = OpenAI(api_key=os.getenv("OPENAI_API_KEY"))

def get_embedding(text: str) -> list[float]:
    response = openai_client.embeddings.create(
        model="text-embedding-3-small",
        input=text
    )
    return response.data[0].embedding

def search_documents(query: str, top_k: int = 5) -> list[dict]:
    query_embedding = get_embedding(query)
    
    result = supabase.rpc("match_chunks", {
        "query_embedding": query_embedding,
        "match_count": top_k
    }).execute()
    
    return result.data if result.data else []

def format_context(chunks: list[dict]) -> str:
    context = ""
    for chunk in chunks:
        source = chunk.get("metadata", {}).get("filename", "unknown")
        content = chunk.get("content", "")
        context += f"\n[Source: {source}]\n{content}\n"
    return context
EOF
Then go to Supabase SQL Editor and run this to recreate the function with exact parameter names matching what supabase-py sends:

sql
drop function if exists match_chunks(vector, int);

create or replace function match_chunks(
  query_embedding vector(1536),
  match_count int default 5
)
returns table(
  id uuid,
  content text,
  metadata jsonb,
  similarity float
)
language sql stable
as $$
  select
    document_chunks.id,
    document_chunks.content,
    document_chunks.metadata,
    1 - (document_chunks.embedding <=> query_embedding) as similarity
  from document_chunks
  order by document_chunks.embedding <=> query_embedding
  limit match_count;
$$;
Note it's language sql not language plpgsql this time — simpler and avoids cache issues.

but how will i create this whole thing like how i know the idea but i am losing on how i will make my stsem intelligent to know how to make arch, were to connect, what to have  etc

10 Jun
This is the right question to ask. Let me explain exactly how the intelligence works.

The system isn't one thing — it's three layers of intelligence stacked on top of each other:

Layer 1 — Your cheat sheets (your brain, hardcoded)
This is where YOU decide what belongs in each domain. For OT, you say "this system always has a DMZ, always has a firewall, always has SCADA at Level 2, always has PLCs at Level 1." Nobody figures this out — you encode it. This is the domain expertise your dad has. You turn that into Python dictionaries. The system doesn't discover this — you write it.

Layer 2 — Your documents (the RAG)
When a user asks for something specific — "what firewall for a pharma plant" — the system searches your documents and finds the answer. The Purdue LLD you just uploaded says "OT Firewall: Stateful + DPI firewall, enforces allowlist-only OT protocol rules." That chunk gets retrieved and fed to the LLM. The LLM doesn't know this — it reads it from your document.

Layer 3 — The LLM (only for language)
The LLM does two narrow jobs. Parse the user's prompt into JSON. Write the explanation prose. It never decides what components go where — that's your cheat sheets. It never invents specs — that's your documents. It only understands language and phrases things.

The connection logic specifically — how does the system know what connects to what?

You define it. In your cheat sheet you define tiers in order. The diagram code connects tier N to tier N+1. For Purdue model you define 6 levels in order — Level 0 at bottom, Level 4 at top — and the diagram draws edges between them following that hierarchy.

The Purdue LLD you just uploaded is the exact blueprint. Look at it:

Level 4/5 → DMZ → Level 3 → Level 2 → Level 1 → Level 0
That sequence becomes your OT diagram layout. Each level has defined components. Each connection has a defined protocol — OPC-UA crosses the DMZ, EtherNet/IP connects Level 1 to Level 2, IO-Link connects Level 0 to Level 1.

You encode that once. Every OT architecture prompt uses it.

Concretely — this is what your OT cheat sheet becomes after this document:

python
"ot": {
    "purdue_levels": {
        "level_4_5": {
            "label": "Enterprise IT",
            "components": ["SAP S/4HANA", "MES Reporting", "BI Analytics"],
            "connects_down_via": "HTTPS/REST"
        },
        "dmz": {
            "label": "Industrial DMZ",
            "components": ["OT Firewall", "Jump Server", "OPC-UA Gateway", "OT SIEM"],
            "connects_down_via": "OPC-UA"
        },
        "level_3": {
            "label": "Site Operations",
            "components": ["MES Cluster", "MOM", "QMS", "Kafka/MQTT Broker"],
            "connects_down_via": "MQTT"
        },
        "level_2": {
            "label": "Supervisory Control",
            "components": ["SCADA Cluster", "Historian", "DCS"],
            "connects_down_via": "EtherNet/IP"
        },
        "level_1": {
            "label": "Machine Control",
            "components": ["PLCs", "Robotics Controllers", "Safety Systems (SIS)"],
            "connects_down_via": "IO-Link"
        },
        "level_0": {
            "label": "Field / Process",
            "components": ["Temperature Sensors", "Vibration Sensors", "Actuators"],
            "connects_down_via": None
        }
    }
}
The diagram renderer reads this top to bottom, draws each level as a zone, puts components inside, draws edges with the protocol label. That's it.

The intelligence isn't magic — it's structured knowledge.

Your job is to take documents like the Purdue LLD and encode the important parts into cheat sheets. That encoding is the product. The LLM just handles the natural language at the edges.

Want to start rewriting the OT cheat sheet properly using this document right now?

Thing 2 — Smarter layout engine
Right now your layout is hardcoded positions. This diagram uses a proper grouping system — parent nodes contain child nodes. React Flow supports this natively with parentId.

Thing 3 — LLM generates the diagram JSON

Good. Here's the full picture of Eraser and where you beat them:

What Eraser is:

At its core is DiagramGPT — uses GPT-4 to generate diagrams from natural language or code inputs in seconds. All diagrams are rendered in Eraser's diagram-as-code format, meaning you can edit them using either code syntax or the visual interface. 
Review my tools

Five diagram types — flowcharts, entity relationship diagrams, cloud architecture diagrams, sequence diagrams, and BPMN diagrams. 
Eraser

Pricing: Free (3 files, 5 AI credits/month), Starter $10/user/month, Business $25/user/month. 
Skypage

Their biggest recent feature — Eraserbot automatically updates architecture diagrams when a pull request modifies a Terraform or Kubernetes file and commits the change back to the PR. 
Skypage

Their weaknesses — where you win:

Learning curve for advanced features and premium pricing for full features. 
SiteOptz

More importantly — things Eraser fundamentally cannot do:

No domain intelligence — it generates generic diagrams. It doesn't know that a pharma plant needs 21 CFR Part 11 audit trails, or that a SCADA system needs a DMZ between IT and OT. You do.
No document grounding — it uses GPT-4's general training. You use your specific corpus of Cisco, ISACA, Purdue LLD, NIST 800-82. Your output is defensible, cited, domain-accurate.
No hybrid/on-prem — Eraser is built for software engineering teams doing cloud/code diagrams. It doesn't handle Cisco routers, PLCs, SCADA, industrial protocols.
No compliance mapping — it draws a diagram, it doesn't tell you what IEC 62443 zone controls you're missing.
Only Enterprise plan customers can upload reference architectures for AI to use — you're giving every user domain-specific intelligence by default. 
Eraser
Your positioning in one line:

Eraser is for software engineers building cloud apps. Basalt is for industrial and enterprise architects building OT, pharma, FMCG, and hybrid systems — with domain-specific intelligence baked in.

What you should copy from Eraser:

The minimal, fast UI — prompt in, diagram out, no friction
Editable diagram after generation — drag nodes, rename, adjust
Export as PNG/SVG
The diagram-as-code idea — show the underlying structure so advanced users can edit it directly
What you don't need to copy:

GitHub integration — not your user's workflow
Collaboration/multiplayer — Phase 2 at earliest
Sequence diagrams, ERDs, flowcharts — stay focused on architecture only

doesnt it use diagram as code something

10 Jun
Yes. That's actually the key insight about how Eraser works under the hood.

Eraser's diagram-as-code approach:

Instead of React Flow nodes and edges, Eraser uses a simple text syntax like this:

cloud-architecture

User > CloudFront [label: HTTPS]
CloudFront > ALB
ALB > EC2-1
ALB > EC2-2
EC2-1 > RDS [label: SQL]
EC2-2 > RDS

group Security {
  WAF
  KMS
  GuardDuty
}
That text renders into a visual diagram. The LLM generates the text, the renderer draws it. Simple and powerful.

Why this is better than what you're building now:

Right now your system hardcodes positions, tier orders, edge connections in Python/React. It's rigid — every new domain needs new layout code.

With diagram-as-code your LLM just outputs text. The renderer handles everything else. Adding a new domain means writing a new cheat sheet prompt, not new rendering code.

For Basalt this means:

User prompt
    ↓
LLM reads prompt + cheat sheet + RAG context
    ↓
LLM outputs diagram-as-code text
    ↓
Your renderer parses it → React Flow diagram
The LLM decides what connects to what, what goes in which group, what labels go on edges. Your cheat sheet is the system prompt that guides those decisions.

Concretely — your LLM prompt becomes:

You are an industrial architecture expert.
Domain: OT / Purdue Model
Cheat sheet: [OT cheat sheet injected here]
Reference context: [RAG chunks injected here]

Generate a diagram-as-code representation of the architecture.
Use this syntax:
- node: ServiceName [type: aws|cisco|scada|sensor]
- edge: A > B [label: protocol]
- group: GroupName { ... nodes ... }

Rules:
- Always include DMZ between IT and OT
- Always connect via OPC-UA across DMZ
- Level 0 at bottom, Level 4 at top
LLM outputs the text. Your renderer draws it.

This is what you should build next — replace the current hardcoded layout with a diagram-as-code pipeline. It's simpler, smarter, and handles any domain automatically.

10 Jun
Here's exactly how to build your own diagram-as-code system with full hybrid/on-prem/cloud support.

Step 1 — Define your own syntax

Eraser has their syntax. You build yours, designed for industrial/enterprise architecture:

// Node types you support
node: AWS::EC2 [label: "App Server", zone: cloud]
node: Cisco::Router [label: "Core Router", zone: onprem]
node: SCADA::Ignition [label: "SCADA Server", zone: onprem]
node: AWS::RDS [label: "Aurora DB", zone: cloud]
node: Generic::Firewall [label: "OT Firewall", zone: dmz]

// Edges with protocol labels
edge: Router > Firewall [label: "VLAN trunk"]
edge: Firewall > SCADA [label: "OPC-UA"]
edge: SCADA > RDS [label: "Direct Connect"]

// Groups / zones
group: "Enterprise IT" [level: 4, color: purple] {
  AWS::SAP
  AWS::RDS
}

group: "Industrial DMZ" [color: orange] {
  Generic::Firewall
  Generic::JumpServer
}

group: "Level 2 - Supervisory" [level: 2, color: blue] {
  SCADA::Ignition
  SCADA::Historian
}

group: "On-Premises Plant Floor" [type: onprem] {
  Cisco::Router
  Cisco::Switch
  PLC::AllenBradley
}

// Compliance tags
compliance: IEC-62443 [applies_to: "Industrial DMZ", "Level 2"]
compliance: 21CFR11 [applies_to: "Enterprise IT"]
Step 2 — Node type library

You define three namespaces:

AWS::       → official AWS services, orange/colored icons
Cisco::     → networking hardware, teal icons  
Generic::   → firewalls, jump servers, gateways
SCADA::     → Ignition, Wonderware, OSIsoft PI
PLC::       → Allen-Bradley, Siemens, Schneider
SAP::       → SAP modules
Sensor::    → field devices, IoT sensors
Step 3 — LLM generates this syntax

Your system prompt to LLM becomes:

You are an industrial architecture expert.
Generate architecture in Basalt diagram syntax.

Domain detected: OT
Deployment: Hybrid (AWS cloud + on-premises plant)
Compliance: IEC 62443, NIST SP 800-82

Cheat sheet rules:
- Always separate IT and OT with Industrial DMZ
- Always use OPC-UA to cross DMZ
- Purdue levels: 0 (field) → 1 (machine) → 2 (supervisory) 
  → 3 (site ops) → DMZ → 4 (enterprise IT) → cloud
- On-prem nodes use Cisco:: or PLC:: namespace
- Cloud nodes use AWS:: namespace
- DMZ nodes use Generic:: namespace

Reference context from documents:
[RAG chunks injected here]

Output ONLY valid Basalt diagram syntax. No explanation.
Step 4 — Your renderer parses it

A simple parser in your frontend reads the syntax and builds React Flow nodes and edges:

AWS::EC2       → orange node, EC2 icon
Cisco::Router  → teal node, router icon
SCADA::Ignition → blue node, SCADA icon
PLC::           → purple node, PLC icon

zone: cloud    → blue dashed boundary box
zone: onprem   → grey solid boundary box  
zone: dmz      → orange dashed boundary box

compliance tag → small badge on the group box
Step 5 — What you get

From one prompt like "pharma OT network hybrid AWS":

LLM outputs your syntax
Parser builds nodes and edges
React Flow renders it
Zones are color coded — cloud blue, on-prem grey, DMZ orange
Compliance badges show on relevant zones
User can drag, edit, add nodes manually after
The full picture:

User prompt
    ↓
LLM #1 → detects domain + deployment type
    ↓
Cheat sheet → injects zone rules + must-haves
    ↓
RAG → injects specific component recommendations
    ↓
LLM #2 → outputs Basalt diagram syntax
    ↓
Parser → converts to React Flow JSON
    ↓
React Flow → renders interactive diagram
    ↓
User edits → drag, add, remove nodes
    ↓
Export → PNG / SVG
Your moat over Eraser:

Eraser's LLM generates generic cloud diagrams with no domain grounding. Your LLM is constrained by your cheat sheets and document corpus — it can't generate a pharma architecture without a DMZ, it can't generate an OT system without Purdue levels, it knows OPC-UA goes across the DMZ and EtherNet/IP stays within Level 1-2.

That constraint is your product. Eraser can never replicate it without building what you're building.

16 Jun
Perfect. Now let me build the icon registry automatically by scanning all SVG files:


Build AWS icon registry
Build AWS icon registry
The path is on your local machine, not my server. Let me give you a script to run locally instead:

bash
cd /Users/SatvikMishra/Desktop/basaltcode
python3 << 'EOF'
import os, json

BASE = "public/icons/aws/Icon-package_04302026/Architecture-Service-Icons_04302026"
registry = {}

for root, dirs, files in os.walk(BASE):
    for file in files:
        if file.endswith("_64.svg") and "@" not in file:
            name = file.replace("Arch_", "").replace("_64.svg", "").replace("-", " ")
            web_path = os.path.join(root, file).replace("public", "")
            registry[name] = web_path

print(f"Found {len(registry)} icons")

with open("public/icons/aws_registry.json", "w") as f:
    json.dump(registry, f, indent=2)

print("Saved to public/icons/aws_registry.json")
EOF
Paste what you see.

16 Jun
Smart. Let's first get all downloadable official icon packs before building any custom ones.

You have AWS done (303 icons). Now let's get Cisco:


Searched the web
Searched the web
Cisco's official icons are freely available from their brand center — you can use them freely but cannot alter them. They come as EPS, JPG, and Visio ZIP formats. 
ResearchGate

The problem is Cisco's official pack is EPS format, not SVG — hard to use directly in a browser. Cisco also has a SAFE Icon Library that includes PNG and SVG files — that's the one we want. 
Rockwell Automation

Go to this URL and download the SAFE icon library:

https://www.cisco.com/c/en/us/solutions/collateral/enterprise/design-zone-security/safe-icon-library.html
Also go to the main topology icons page and download the Visio ZIP (it contains usable PNG files):

https://www.cisco.com/c/en/us/about/brand-center/network-topology-icons.html
While you check those, here's the honest reality — Cisco's free pack is limited and awkward to use in web apps. Most professional diagram tools (including Eraser) actually use clean SVG recreations of Cisco icons rather than the official pack.

So the plan is:

Download now (free official):

AWS ✅ done — 303 SVGs
Cisco SAFE library — try the link above
Azure — free from Microsoft at https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/architecture/icons/
GCP — free from Google
Build custom SVGs for:

SAP, PLC, SCADA, Industrial — we already built 43 of these
Try downloading the Cisco SAFE library and Azure icons now. Tell me what you find at those links.

bash
cd /Users/SatvikMishra/Desktop/basaltcode
python3 << 'EOF'
import os, json

BASE = "public/icons/cisco/SAFE Icons Library"
registry = {}

for root, dirs, files in os.walk(BASE):
    for file in files:
        if file.endswith(".svg"):
            name = file.replace("Design_38_", "").replace("Capability_43_", "").replace("Capability_16_", "").replace("Design OL_38_", "").replace(".svg", "").strip()
            web_path = os.path.join(root, file).replace("public", "")
            registry[f"cisco::{name}"] = web_path

print(f"Found {len(registry)} Cisco icons")
with open("public/icons/cisco_registry.json", "w") as f:
    json.dump(registry, f, indent=2)
print("Saved")
EOF

now go for registry creation

16 Jun
Run this — it builds one unified registry combining AWS + Cisco + all custom icons:

bash
cd /Users/SatvikMishra/Desktop/basaltcode
python3 << 'EOF'
import os, json

registry = {}

# AWS icons
aws_base = "public/icons/aws/Icon-package_04302026/Architecture-Service-Icons_04302026"
for root, dirs, files in os.walk(aws_base):
    for file in files:
        if file.endswith("_64.svg") and "@" not in file:
            name = file.replace("Arch_", "").replace("_64.svg", "").replace("-", " ")
            web_path = os.path.join(root, file).replace("public", "")
            registry[f"aws::{name}"] = web_path

print(f"AWS: {len(registry)} icons")

# Cisco SAFE icons
cisco_base = "public/icons/cisco/SAFE Icons Library"
cisco_count = 0
for root, dirs, files in os.walk(cisco_base):
    for file in files:
        if file.endswith(".svg"):
            name = file.replace("Design_38_", "").replace("Capability_43_", "").replace("Capability_16_", "").replace("Design OL_38_", "").replace(".svg", "").strip()
            web_path = os.path.join(root, file).replace("public", "")
            key = f"cisco::{name}"
            if key not in registry:
                registry[key] = web_path
                cisco_count += 1

print(f"Cisco: {cisco_count} icons")

# Custom icons (SAP, PLC, SCADA, Industrial, Generic)
custom_namespaces = ["sap", "plc", "scada", "industrial", "generic"]
custom_count = 0
for ns in custom_namespaces:
    ns_dir = f"public/icons/{ns}"
    if os.path.exists(ns_dir):
        for file in os.listdir(ns_dir):
            if file.endswith(".svg"):
                name = file.replace(".svg", "").replace("_", " ").title()
                web_path = f"/icons/{ns}/{file}"
                registry[f"{ns}::{name}"] = web_path
                custom_count += 1

print(f"Custom: {custom_count} icons")
print(f"Total: {len(registry)} icons")

with open("public/icons/registry.json", "w") as f:
    json.dump(registry, f, indent=2)

print("Saved to public/icons/registry.json")
EOF
Paste what you see.

done with aws and cisco separate registry now what icons turn

import os, json

registry = {}

# AWS
aws_base = "public/icons/aws/Icon-package_04302026/Architecture-Service-Icons_04302026"
for root, dirs, files in os.walk(aws_base):
    for file in files:
        if file.endswith("_64.svg") and "@" not in file:
            name = file.replace("Arch_", "").replace("_64.svg", "").replace("-", " ")
            web_path = os.path.join(root, file).replace("public", "")
            registry[f"aws::{name}"] = web_path

# Cisco
cisco_base = "public/icons/cisco/SAFE Icons Library"
for root, dirs, files in os.walk(cisco_base):
    for file in files:
        if file.endswith(".svg"):
            name = file.replace("Design_38_", "").replace("Capability_43_", "").replace("Capability_16_", "").replace("Design OL_38_", "").replace(".svg", "").strip()

import os, json

    registry = json.load(f)

before = len(registry)

# Azure icons
azure_base = "public/icons/azure/Azure_Public_Service_Icons/Icons"
for root, dirs, files in os.walk(azure_base):
    for file in files:
        if file.endswith(".svg"):

            import re

what is defender PLC

16 Jun
Defender for IoT is Microsoft Azure's security product specifically for industrial/OT networks. It monitors and protects industrial devices.

The "Defender PLC" icon represents a PLC (Programmable Logic Controller) being monitored by Microsoft Defender for IoT. Same for Defender RTU, Defender DCS, Defender HMI etc — they're all industrial device icons from that security product.

The icons themselves are just clean device illustrations — a PLC icon looks like a PLC, an RTU icon looks like an RTU. The "Defender" prefix just means they're from that product's icon set.

For Basalt these are perfect — you can use them as:

azure::Defender PLC → PLC node in your diagram
azure::Defender RTU → RTU node
azure::Defender DCS Controller → DCS node
azure::Defender HMI → HMI node
azure::Defender Sensor → Sensor node
azure::Defender Industrial Robot → Robot node
azure::Defender Historian → Historian node
This means you don't need to build custom PLC/SCADA icons at all — Azure already has them professionally designed as SVGs.

Now run the Azure registry update command I gave earlier. Tell me the total icon count after adding Azure.

ohk so they are azure defined logo for plc from their services

16 Jun
Not exactly. They're not Azure cloud services — they're icons that represent physical industrial devices that Azure Defender for IoT can discover and monitor on your plant network.

Think of it this way:

Microsoft built Defender for IoT to protect industrial networks. To show customers what devices are on their network, they needed icons for physical hardware — PLCs, RTUs, HMIs, sensors, robots. So they designed professional SVG icons for all of them.

The icons themselves are just clean illustrations of the physical devices. A PLC icon looks like a real PLC panel. An RTU looks like an RTU box. They have nothing to do with Azure cloud — they just happen to be in the Azure icon pack because that's the product that uses them.

For Basalt this is a gift — you get professionally designed industrial device icons for free, officially from Microsoft, in SVG format. Your diagram can show a real-looking PLC node, HMI node, sensor node without you having to design anything.

So when you use azure::Defender PLC in a Basalt diagram node, the user sees a clean professional PLC icon — it doesn't imply Azure cloud at all. You just rename the key in your registry to industrial::PLC and it's yours.

Run this to remap them properly:

bash
cd /Users/SatvikMishra/Desktop/basaltcode
python3 << 'EOF'
import json

with open("public/icons/registry.json") as f:
    registry = json.load(f)

# Remap Defender industrial icons to industrial namespace
remaps = {
    "azure::Defender PLC": "industrial::PLC",
    "azure::Defender RTU": "industrial::RTU",
    "azure::Defender HMI": "industrial::HMI",
    "azure::Defender Historian": "industrial::Historian",
    "azure::Defender Sensor": "industrial::Sensor",
    "azure::Defender DCS Controller": "industrial::DCS Controller",
    "azure::Defender Industrial Robot": "industrial::Industrial Robot",
    "azure::Defender Engineering Station": "industrial::Engineering Station",
    "azure::Defender Industrial Printer": "industrial::Industrial Printer",
    "azure::Defender Industrial Scale System": "industrial::Industrial Scale",
    "azure::Defender Pneumatic Device": "industrial::Pneumatic Device",
    "azure::Defender Relay": "industrial::Relay",
    "azure::Defender Meter": "industrial::Meter",
}

count = 0
for old_key, new_key in remaps.items():
    if old_key in registry:
        registry[new_key] = registry[old_key]
        count += 1
        print(f"  {old_key} → {new_key}")

print(f"\nRemapped {count} industrial icons")
print(f"Total: {len(registry)} icons")

with open("public/icons/registry.json", "w") as f:
    json.dump(registry, f, indent=2)
EOF

Good additions. Let me check which of these have free downloadable icon packs:


Searched the web
Searched the web
Here's the honest picture:

CrowdStrike, Rapid7, Sophos — these are vendor logos, not device icons. They exist as SVG logos on sites like worldvectorlogo.com but there's an important distinction:

Logos = brand mark of the company (the falcon, the R7, the Sophos S)
Architecture icons = represent a service/product in a diagram
For architecture diagrams you don't use vendor logos — you use capability icons. For example instead of the CrowdStrike falcon logo you'd use a "EDR" or "Endpoint Detection" icon, then label it "CrowdStrike Falcon."

Dell switches/servers — Dell has no free official SVG icon pack for architecture diagrams. Same for most hardware vendors.

PLC/SCADA/HMI individual — you already have these from Azure Defender icons and our custom SVGs.

Defense in Depth — this is a concept/pattern, not a single icon. It's represented as concentric zone layers in a diagram.

My recommendation — build these as custom branded SVGs. I can make clean branded icons for CrowdStrike, Rapid7, Sophos, Dell right now. Same style as the others — colored square, white illustration, label. Want me to build them?

study websites like this in detil to get the best of how to make my webiste although mine is better becz i have templates adn sme with me so websites like eraser.io and such websites

16 Jun
Let me study them properly:


Searched the web
Searched the web
Now I have a thorough picture. Here's what you need to know about every competitor and exactly how Basalt beats them:

Eraser.io

Uses GPT-4 and diagram-as-code with its own proprietary syntax — simple enough that non-technical users can grasp it quickly. Their biggest recent feature is Eraserbot, which automatically updates architecture diagrams when a Terraform or Kubernetes file is modified in a pull request. 
GitHub

Only 5 diagram types — flowcharts, ER diagrams, cloud architecture, sequence diagrams, and BPMN. No industrial, no OT, no pharma, no hybrid on-prem. 
IconScout

Where you beat them: Eraser is built for software engineers doing cloud-native apps. It has zero industrial domain knowledge. No Purdue model, no IEC 62443 zones, no SCADA, no PLC. Your templates and SME access is exactly what they lack.

Cloudcraft

Professional cloud architecture diagramming for AWS and Azure only. Supports hundreds of native cloud components, provides both 2D and 3D perspectives, and can automatically scan and import your live AWS account to generate diagrams. 
Eraser

Starts at $49/user/month — expensive for individuals. 
Logo

Where you beat them: Cloudcraft is pure cloud. No on-prem, no Cisco, no industrial devices, no hybrid. Their auto-import of AWS accounts is clever but irrelevant to industrial architects designing new systems.

Lucidchart

General purpose diagramming — medium quality AI generation, good for flowcharts but less capable for complex architecture. No native diagram-as-code support. 
GitHub

Where you beat them: Too generic. No domain intelligence whatsoever. An industrial engineer gets no value from it that they couldn't get from Draw.io for free.

Miro

65M+ users, excellent collaboration, infinite canvas, best-in-class for workshops and brainstorming. Recently expanded into technical diagramming with smart containers and custom shape packs. 
iconLogoVector

Where you beat them: Miro is a collaboration whiteboard that added diagramming as a feature. It's not purpose-built for architecture. No domain intelligence, no compliance grounding, no industrial icons.

What every competitor is missing — your gap:

None of them understand:

Purdue model levels
IEC 62443 security zones
OT/IT convergence patterns
Pharma validation zones
SCADA/PLC/HMI as first-class diagram components
Compliance controls mapped to diagram zones
Hybrid on-prem + cloud in one diagram
What you should steal from each of them:

From Eraser — simple clean UI, left panel for prompt, right panel for canvas, diagram-as-code underneath, fast generation

Now let's build it. This is the brain of Basalt:

Ran a command, read a file
Download and save to backend/diagram_generator.py.

Now add the generate endpoint to main.py. Open backend/main.py and add at the top with other imports:

python
from diagram_generator import generate_diagram
Then add this endpoint at the bottom:

python
@app.post("/generate")
async def generate(body: dict):
    prompt = body.get("prompt", "")

    # Step 1: parse
    parse_res = await parse_prompt({"prompt": prompt})
    parsed = parse_res["parsed"]

    # Step 2: classify
    classify_res = await classify_prompt({"parsed": parsed})
    classified = classify_res["classified"]
    domain = classified["app_type"]

    # Step 3: RAG search
    search_query = f"{domain} architecture {' '.join(parsed.get('compliance', []))}"
    search_res = await search({"query": search_query, "top_k": 8})
    rag_context = search_res["context"]

    # Step 4: generate diagram
    result = generate_diagram(prompt, domain, classified, rag_context)

    return {
        "status": result["status"],
        "parsed": parsed,
        "classified": classified,
        "diagram": result.get("diagram"),
        "error": result.get("error")
    }
Save and test:

bash
curl -X POST http://localhost:8000/generate \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"prompt": "OT network for pharma plant with IEC 62443 compliance hybrid AWS"}'

Let's fix the icons first — that's the most important. The issue is the icon URL path is wrong. Let me fix IconNode.tsx.

Open app/components/diagram/IconNode.tsx and replace the entire getIconUrl function with this:

tsx
function getIconUrl(iconKey: string): string | null {
  if (!iconKey) return null;
  const parts = iconKey.split("::");
  if (parts.length !== 2) return null;
  const [ns, name] = parts;

  if (["sap","plc","scada","industrial","generic","security","hardware","patterns"].includes(ns)) {
    const filename = name.toLowerCase().replace(/ /g, "_").replace(/-/g, "_") + ".svg";
    return `/icons/${ns}/${filename}`;
  }

  if (ns === "cisco") {
    const filename = `Design_38_${name}.svg`;
    return `/icons/cisco/SAFE Icons Library/Design Icons (purple) .33x.38/SVG/${filename}`;
  }

  if (ns === "aws") {
    const cleanName = name.replace(/ /g, "-");
    return `/icons/aws/Icon-package_04302026/Architecture-Service-Icons_04302026/Arch_Compute/64/Arch_${cleanName}_64.svg`;
  }

  if (ns === "azure") {
    return null;
  }

  return null;
}
Save and test. Tell me if icons start showing.

which llm i am currenlty using

16 Jun
You're using gpt-4o-mini from OpenAI.

It's set in backend/diagram_generator.py in the generate_diagram function:

python
response = client.chat.completions.create(
    model="gpt-4o-mini",
    ...
)
gpt-4o-mini is OpenAI's cheap fast model — good for getting started and keeping costs low while you build. It's producing solid diagram JSON as you've seen.

When you want higher quality output later (more accurate component selection, better reasoning about your documents), you can switch to gpt-4o or gpt-4-turbo by changing that one line. But for Stage 1 development, gpt-4o-mini is the right choice — fast and nearly free.

I have an idea of an ai based system architecture builder and I am trying t make it so how can I make a system that is developed and learned on the docs I provide to it ranging from Cisco, sap, Allen Bradley, crew, isc2, isa, Isaca docs how can I What you're describing is essentially an AI-powered architecture engineer/copilot that can ingest technical documentation from multiple vendors and standards bodies, learn from them, and generate system architectures, diagrams, recommendations, and compliance checks. This is a realistic project, but you should think of it as RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation) + Knowledge Graph + Reasoning Agents, rather than "training an AI from scratch."

This is a solid technical breakdown, and it overlaps almost exactly with what you've already built. Let me map it to where you actually are so it's useful rather than overwhelming.

What this person recommends vs what you've already done:

The RAG-not-fine-tuning advice — you already did this. Supabase pgvector, 657 chunks, OpenAI embeddings. Done.

Document ingestion pipeline — done. You have ingest.py chunking and embedding PDFs.

Multi-agent architecture — you deliberately chose NOT to do this, and you were right. Your pivot decision was "two LLM jobs only: parse and generate, everything else deterministic." Adding CrewAI/LangGraph multi-agent orchestration right now would slow you down massively for Stage 1, which is just about beautiful diagrams. Skip it.

Diagram generation as JSON then render — done. Your diagram_generator.py outputs JSON, React Flow renders it.

Rule engine for compliance — this is your Stage 2 (the consistency validator in your roadmap). Not now.

Knowledge graph (Neo4j) — this is genuinely useful but it's a Stage 2/3 thing. It would let you answer "what Cisco switch supports PROFINET and connects to Allen-Bradley." Powerful, but not needed for diagram generation. Don't add it yet.

The one genuinely useful new idea here:

Metadata on chunks. This person emphasizes it and they're right. Right now your chunks are just text. If you tag each chunk with {vendor, domain, document_type, standard}, your RAG retrieval gets far more precise — an OT query only pulls OT chunks. That's already in your Week 3 roadmap. It's the highest-value upgrade from this whole list.

My honest advice:

This recommendation is describing your Stage 3 end state — the full "AI Solutions Architect." It's a good north star but if you try to build all of it now (knowledge graph + multi-agent + rule engine + hybrid retrieval) you'll never ship Stage 1.

You're 80% done with Stage 1. Finish the icon rendering, polish the layout, deploy it. Then add metadata-filtered RAG. Then consider the knowledge graph in Stage 2.

Don't let a comprehensive technical plan derail a nearly-finished MVP. Ship first.

Want to get back to fixing the icons so we can finish Stage 1?

LLMs

Best proprietary: [OpenAI Platform](https://platform.openai.com/?utm_source=chatgpt.com) (GPT-5 family)
Best open source: [Meta Llama Models](https://www.llama.com/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
Strong reasoning alternative: [Qwen Models](https://qwenlm.github.io/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
Agent Framework

Use LangGraph.
Avoid CrewAI for enterprise-scale orchestration.

                     ↓

               Retrieval
                     ↓

         Architecture Generation

    raise IEC62443Violation()

gRPC between services — you have one backend, there are no services to connect
Kubernetes + H100/B200 GPU cluster — this is enterprise-scale infrastructure. You're running on localhost. Railway + Vercel will handle you fine until you have thousands of users. A GPU cluster costs more per month than your entire venture needs right now.
Fine-tuning — they even say this comes last. Correct. Maybe never.
Switching vector DBs (Qdrant/Milvus/Weaviate) — you already have Supabase pgvector working. Don't migrate. It handles millions of vectors fine.
Actually worth doing (and already in your roadmap):

Metadata on chunks → better RAG (Week 3)
Deterministic rules / validator → catches LLM mistakes (Week 4, Stage 2)
Keep using OpenAI → you're on gpt-4o-mini, fine for now

Both these recommendations describe the same thing — a maximal enterprise architecture for a mature, funded, multi-engineer company. They're not wrong as a long-term vision. But you are one person trying to ship Stage 1: a beautiful diagram generator. None of this changes what you need to do this week.

16 Jun
Let me be straight with you, because this is an important decision point.

The architectures aren't great right now — but the cause is not your vector database or your parsing library. Switching Supabase→Chroma or pdfplumber→Unstructured won't fix it. Here's the actual diagnosis:

Why your diagrams are mediocre right now:

The LLM barely uses your documents. Look at your /generate flow — it does ONE generic RAG search with query like "ot architecture IEC 62443" and stuffs 8 chunks in. The LLM mostly ignores them and generates from its own training knowledge. That's why you get generic Allen-Bradley/Siemens output that doesn't reflect your specific CPwE document.
No metadata filtering. Your OT query searches all 657 chunks including AWS and pharma docs. Noise drowns the signal.
gpt-4o-mini is weak at structure. It follows your document context loosely.
Chunking is too coarse. 800-word chunks mean a single chunk mixes multiple concepts, so retrieval is imprecise.
None of those are fixed by Chroma or Unstructured. Chroma is the same kind of vector store as Supabase pgvector — moving sideways. Unstructured parses PDFs slightly better but your text extraction isn't the bottleneck.

What actually fixes it — in order of impact:

Better chunking + metadata — chunk the CPwE doc by section, tag each chunk {domain: ot, source: cpwe, zone: ...}. Then OT queries pull only OT chunks. Big win.
Make the LLM actually cite the documents — change the prompt so it MUST use components/protocols/zones found in the retrieved chunks, not its own knowledge. Force grounding.
Multiple targeted RAG searches — instead of one vague query, search separately for "zones", "components", "protocols", "security controls" and combine. Much richer context.
Upgrade to gpt-4o for generation — better at following document grounding.
So the honest answer: keep Supabase, keep your parser. The improvement you want comes from how you retrieve and how you prompt, not which libraries you swap in.

Want to start by ingesting the CPwE doc properly with section-based chunking and metadata, then rewrite the generation prompt to force document grounding? That's what will actually make the diagrams good.

(venv) SatvikMishra@MacBook-Pro ~/Desktop/basaltcode % cat /Users/SatvikMishra/Desktop/basaltcode/backend/ingest.py
import os
import sys
from dotenv import load_dotenv
from supabase import create_client
from openai import OpenAI
import pypdf
import json
load_dotenv()
supabase = create_client(os.getenv("SUPABASE_URL"), os.getenv("SUPABASE_KEY"))
openai_client = OpenAI(api_key=os.getenv("OPENAI_API_KEY"))
CHUNK_SIZE = 800
CHUNK_OVERLAP = 100
def extract_text_from_pdf(pdf_path: str) -> str:
    text = ""
    with open(pdf_path, "rb") as f:
        reader = pypdf.PdfReader(f)
        for page in reader.pages:
            page_text = page.extract_text()
            if page_text:
                text += page_text + "\n"
    return text
def chunk_text(text: str, chunk_size: int = CHUNK_SIZE, overlap: int = CHUNK_OVERLAP) -> list[str]:
    words = text.split()
    chunks = []
    start = 0
    while start < len(words):
        end = start + chunk_size
        chunk = " ".join(words[start:end])
        chunks.append(chunk)
        start += chunk_size - overlap
    return chunks
def get_embedding(text: str) -> list[float]:
    response = openai_client.embeddings.create(
        model="text-embedding-3-small",
        input=text
    )
    return response.data[0].embedding
def ingest_document(pdf_path: str, source_name: str = None):
    filename = os.path.basename(pdf_path)
    source = source_name or filename

    print(f"\nIngesting: {filename}")

    # check if already ingested
    existing = supabase.table("documents").select("id").eq("name", filename).execute()
    if existing.data:
        print(f"  Already ingested, skipping.")
        return
    # extract text
    print(f"  Extracting text...")
    text = extract_text_from_pdf(pdf_path)
    if not text.strip():
        print(f"  No text found, skipping.")
        return
    print(f"  Extracted {len(text)} characters")
    # save document record
    doc_result = supabase.table("documents").insert({
        "name": filename,
        "source": source
    }).execute()
    doc_id = doc_result.data[0]["id"]
    print(f"  Document ID: {doc_id}")
    # chunk text
    chunks = chunk_text(text)
    print(f"  Created {len(chunks)} chunks")
    # embed and store each chunk
    for i, chunk in enumerate(chunks):
        if not chunk.strip():
            continue

        embedding = get_embedding(chunk)

        supabase.table("document_chunks").insert({
            "document_id": doc_id,
            "content": chunk,
            "embedding": embedding,
            "metadata": {"chunk_index": i, "source": source, "filename": filename}
        }).execute()

        if (i + 1) % 10 == 0:
            print(f"  Stored {i + 1}/{len(chunks)} chunks...")
    print(f"  Done. {len(chunks)} chunks stored.")
def ingest_all(documents_folder: str):
    pdfs = [f for f in os.listdir(documents_folder) if f.endswith(".pdf")]

    skip = ["Satvik_Mishra_Novartis_HealthIntelligence.pdf"]
    pdfs = [p for p in pdfs if p not in skip]

    print(f"Found {len(pdfs)} PDFs to ingest")

    for pdf_file in pdfs:
        full_path = os.path.join(documents_folder, pdf_file)
        ingest_document(full_path)

    print("\nAll documents ingested.")
if __name__ == "__main__":
    documents_dir = os.path.join(os.path.dirname(__file__), "documents")
    ingest_all(documents_dir)%

which model i am using text embedding-----

16 Jun
You're using text-embedding-3-small from OpenAI.

It's in ingest.py in the get_embedding function:

python
response = openai_client.embeddings.create(
    model="text-embedding-3-small",
    input=text
)
This produces 1536-dimension vectors, which matches your Supabase schema (vector(1536)).

It's OpenAI's efficient embedding model — cheap and fast, good quality. The bigger option is text-embedding-3-large (3072 dimensions, more accurate, ~6x cost) but if you switched to it you'd have to change your database schema to vector(3072) and re-embed everything.

For your scale, text-embedding-3-small is the right choice. Don't change it. The retrieval quality gains you want come from metadata filtering and better chunking, not a bigger embedding model.

Run the ingest command and paste what you see.

import os
from dotenv import load_dotenv
from supabase import create_client
from openai import OpenAI
load_dotenv()
supabase = create_client(os.getenv("SUPABASE_URL"), os.getenv("SUPABASE_KEY"))
openai_client = OpenAI(api_key=os.getenv("OPENAI_API_KEY"))
def get_embedding(text: str) -> list[float]:
    response = openai_client.embeddings.create(
        model="text-embedding-3-small",
        input=text
    )
    return response.data[0].embedding
def search_documents(query: str, top_k: int = 5) -> list[dict]:
    query_embedding = get_embedding(query)

    result = supabase.rpc("match_chunks", {
        "query_embedding": query_embedding,
        "match_count": top_k
    }).execute()

    return result.data if result.data else []
def format_context(chunks: list[dict]) -> str:
    context = ""
    for chunk in chunks:
        source = chunk.get("metadata", {}).get("filename", "unknown")
        content = chunk.get("content", "")
        context += f"\n[Source: {source}]\n{content}\n"
    return context

16 Jun
That's a real and important point — CPwE documents are full of architecture diagrams, and those images carry the actual topology that the text only describes loosely. Right now your pipeline only reads text (pypdf extracts text, ignores images entirely).

Here's the honest picture of what it takes to use the images, and whether you should do it now.

What "studying the pictures" actually requires:

The images in your PDFs are architecture diagrams. To make them useful you'd need a vision model (like GPT-4o with vision) to look at each diagram and describe it in text — "Enterprise zone at top connects via dual firewall to Industrial DMZ, which connects to Level 3 containing MES and Historian..." — then you embed that description into your RAG alongside the text chunks.

The pipeline becomes:

Extract each page's images (PyMuPDF can pull them out)
Send each diagram image to GPT-4o vision → get a text description
Embed that description as a chunk tagged {type: diagram_description}
Now RAG retrieves diagram knowledge too
This is genuinely valuable — the diagrams in CPwE are the most information-dense part. But it's also more cost (vision API calls per image) and more complexity.

My honest recommendation: This is a Week 3 enhancement, not a right-now thing. You're in the middle of fixing the OT structure rules. Finish that first — get one domain producing correct diagrams from text — then come back and add vision-based diagram extraction as a clear upgrade.

This is a real, defensible OT architecture. Well done.

Now your question — OT/IT integration. This is the heart of CPwE and it's exactly what the IDMZ provides. Here's how the integration actually works, and how to make Basalt show it:

The integration happens through the IDMZ — and that's the whole point. OT and IT never talk directly. Everything crosses the DMZ through controlled, brokered connections:

Enterprise → DMZ → Level 3 — the SAP ERP needs production data. It doesn't reach into the plant. Instead, the OPC-UA Gateway in the DMZ pulls data from the Site Historian (Level 3), and the ERP reads from the gateway. The data is "brokered" — terminated and re-originated at the DMZ.
The two firewalls enforce it — Palo Alto (enterprise-facing) only allows specific traffic from IT into the DMZ. Fortinet (industrial-facing) only allows specific traffic from the DMZ into OT. Nothing passes straight through.
Jump Server — when an IT admin needs to access an OT device, they connect to the Jump Server in the DMZ first, then from there into OT. Never directly.
What's slightly off in your current diagram for true IT/OT integration:

Right now the connections show Enterprise components linking down, but the data flow story isn't explicit. For a complete OT/IT integration view you want to add:

An MES at Level 3 (currently missing) — MES is the key IT/OT bridge. It takes production orders from ERP (IT) and sends them to PLCs (OT)
A clear data flow: ERP → (DMZ broker) → MES → SCADA → PLC for "production order down", and Sensor → PLC → Historian → (DMZ broker) → ERP for "production data up"
Want me to update the OT rules so it always includes the MES bridge at Level 3 and shows the bidirectional IT/OT data flow (orders down, telemetry up)? That's what makes it a true integration diagram rather than just a layered stack.

What it does:

(venv) SatvikMishra@MacBook-Pro ~/Desktop/basaltcode % sed -n '/def multi_search/,/return combined/p' /Users/SatvikMishra/Desktop/basaltcode/backend/search.py
def multi_search(domain: str, compliance: list, top_k: int = 4) -> list[dict]:
    """Run several targeted searches and combine — richer context than one vague query."""
    queries = [
        f"{domain} architecture zones and network segmentation",
        f"{domain} components devices PLC SCADA controllers servers",
        f"{domain} protocols ports communication",
        f"{domain} security controls {' '.join(compliance)}",
    ]
    seen_ids = set()
    combined = []
    for q in queries:
        results = search_documents(q, top_k=top_k, domain=domain)
        for r in results:
            rid = r.get("id") or r.get("content", "")[:50]
            if rid not in seen_ids:
                seen_ids.add(rid)
                combined.append(r)
    return combined

Let me give you an upgraded multi_search that adds a diagram-knowledge query and pulls real reference architectures from those 95 CPwE diagrams:


Create upgraded multi_search
Create upgraded multi_search
Now replace the multi_search function in your search.py. Open backend/search.py, find the entire def multi_search(...) function (from def multi_search down to its return combined line), delete it, and paste this in its place:

python
def multi_search(domain: str, compliance: list, top_k: int = 4) -> list[dict]:
    """Run several targeted searches and combine — richer context than one vague query.
    Includes a dedicated query for extracted diagram knowledge (the golden reference architectures)."""
    queries = [
        f"{domain} architecture zones levels network segmentation",
        f"{domain} components devices PLC SCADA controllers switches servers product names",
        f"{domain} protocols ports communication OPC-UA EtherNet/IP",
        f"{domain} security controls firewall DMZ {' '.join(compliance)}",
        f"{domain} reference architecture diagram topology Stratix Catalyst FactoryTalk Cell Area Zone",
    ]
    seen_ids = set()
    combined = []
    diagram_chunks = []

    for q in queries:
        results = search_documents(q, top_k=top_k, domain=domain)
        for r in results:
            rid = r.get("id") or r.get("content", "")[:50]
            if rid not in seen_ids:
                seen_ids.add(rid)
                if r.get("metadata", {}).get("type") == "diagram_description":
                    diagram_chunks.append(r)
                else:
                    combined.append(r)

    return diagram_chunks + combined

SYSTEM_PROMPT = """You are an expert industrial and enterprise architecture designer.
Your job is to generate architecture diagrams as structured JSON.
You have access to {icon_count} icons. Use ONLY these icon keys (pick the most appropriate):
{icon_sample}
RULES:
1. Every node MUST have an icon key from the list above
2. Use real product names from the domain (Ignition SCADA, OSIsoft PI, Allen-Bradley PLC etc)
3. Group nodes into zones/groups that make architectural sense
4. Every edge must have a label showing the protocol or relationship
5. Follow the domain rules provided exactly
6. Output ONLY valid JSON, no explanation, no markdown
OUTPUT FORMAT:
{{
  "title": "Architecture title",
  "nodes": [
    {{
      "id": "unique_id",
      "label": "Component Name",
      "icon": "namespace::IconName",
      "zone": "zone_id",
      "description": "What this component does and why it is here"
    }}
  ],
  "edges": [
    {{
      "id": "edge_id",
      "from": "node_id",
      "to": "node_id",
      "label": "Protocol or relationship",
      "style": "solid or dashed"
    }}
  ],
  "zones": [
    {{
      "id": "zone_id",
      "label": "Zone Label",
      "type": "cloud or onprem or dmz or safety or enterprise",
      "color": "blue or grey or orange or red or purple",
      "nodes": ["node_id_list"]
    }}
  ]
}}"""
def get_icon_sample(domain: str) -> str:
    relevant_prefixes = {
        "ot": ["industrial::", "scada::", "plc::", "cisco::", "security::", "patterns::", "aws::"],
        "pharma": ["aws::", "azure::", "security::", "generic::", "sap::"],
        "fmcg": ["industrial::", "scada::", "plc::", "sap::", "cisco::", "security::", "patterns::"],
        "scada": ["scada::", "industrial::", "plc::", "cisco::", "security::"],
        "metal": ["industrial::", "scada::", "plc::", "sap::", "cisco::"],
        "general": ["aws::", "azure::", "cisco::", "generic::", "security::"]
    }
    prefixes = relevant_prefixes.get(domain, relevant_prefixes["general"])
    sample = []
    for prefix in prefixes:
        keys = [k for k in ICON_KEYS if k.startswith(prefix)][:8]
        sample.extend(keys)
    return "\n".join(sample[:60])
def generate_diagram(prompt: str, domain: str, classified: dict, rag_context: str) -> dict:
    domain_rules = DOMAIN_RULES.get(domain, DOMAIN_RULES["general"])
    icon_sample = get_icon_sample(domain)
    system = SYSTEM_PROMPT.format(
        icon_count=len(ICON_KEYS),
        icon_sample=icon_sample
    )
    user_message = f"""Generate an architecture diagram for:
{prompt}
DOMAIN: {domain.upper()}
CLOUD: {classified.get('cloud', 'aws')}
COMPLIANCE: {', '.join(classified.get('compliance', []))}
DOMAIN RULES TO FOLLOW:
{domain_rules}
REFERENCE CONTEXT FROM STANDARDS DOCUMENTS:
{rag_context}
Generate a complete, detailed architecture diagram JSON following the rules above.
Use specific product names. Include all necessary components for a production system.
Every node must have a real icon key from the list provided."""
    response = client.chat.completions.create(
        model="gpt-4o-mini",
        messages=[
            {"role": "system", "content": system},
            {"role": "user", "content": user_message}
        ],
        max_tokens=3000,
        temperature=0.3

17 Jun
Good choice — that's the Eraser look and it's the most readable for plant architectures. This is a bigger change because it needs a new diagram structure (cells as a concept) plus parser rendering for nested side-by-side stacks.

Let me think through the cleanest approach before writing code, because this touches three things:

LLM output — needs a new cells concept in the JSON (each cell has a name + its own L2/L1/L0 nodes)
OT rules — instruct the LLM to generate cells for plant-type prompts
Parser — render the upper levels (Enterprise, DMZ, Level 3) as full-width bands, then Level 2/1/0 as side-by-side cell columns
Here's the design I'll build:

[ Enterprise Zone ]        ← full-width band
[ Industrial DMZ ]         ← full-width band
[ Level 3 Site Ops ]       ← full-width band
┌─ Cell/Area Zones ──────────────────────────┐
│ [Body Shop]  [Paint Shop]  [Final Assembly] │  ← side-by-side cells
│  L2 HMI       L2 HMI         L2 HMI          │
│  L1 PLC       L1 PLC         L1 PLC          │
│  L0 Robots    L0 Booth       L0 Conveyor     │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────┘
The top half stays as your clean full-width bands. The bottom becomes a Cell/Area container with vertical cell columns inside.

Honest answer: this is a clean PNG export (notice it's the exported image — the toolbar/sidebar are gone), and it now matches Eraser's completeness — Level 5 with Internet + Perimeter FW, Level 4, full IDMZ, rich Level 3, three balanced cells. Excellent result.

Now your real question: did we overfit to this one prompt, or will it generalize?

The honest truth is a bit of both, and here's the precise breakdown:

What generalizes well (works for any prompt):

The parser (full-width bands, cells, arbitrary zones) — pure rendering logic, prompt-independent. We proved this: AWS, Pharma, OT all rendered.
Domain detection, icon loading, editability, export — all generic.
The grounding/RAG — works for any query.
What is somewhat tuned to OT/automotive:

The OT domain rules now hardcode specific levels (Level 5/4/3), specific components (Patch/WSUS, AV/Defender), and the automotive cell example (Body Shop/Paint Shop/Assembly).
So here's the nuance: the OT rules will produce great results for any OT/industrial prompt — a different prompt like "OT network for a food packaging plant" will still get the Level 5/4/DMZ/3 structure, and the LLM will adapt the cells to that plant (Mixing/Filling/Packaging instead of Body/Paint/Assembly). It's not overfit to the exact words "automotive" — it's tuned to the OT domain, which is correct and intended.

But it IS specialized to OT. A pure cloud or pharma prompt uses different (lighter) rules, so those won't be as deeply structured as OT yet — that's why your AWS/Pharma outputs were good but simpler.
